People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, Dr. Smith. Yeah, well, to be completely honest, I'm not entirely sure where to start, because it's all just been so confusing lately. I work as a bookkeeping clerk—just doing basic accounting and auditing stuff since I finished high school, and I'm taking a couple classes too—and normally I'm really good at my job, very detail-oriented, you know? But recently, I've started doing these things at work and at school that just... they completely do not make sense. Even to me. 

Like, I'll be sitting at my desk with a stack of expense reports, and instead of just entering the data into the spreadsheet like a normal person, I'll take all the little paper receipts and start lining them up, end to end, all the way across the office floor. I don't even really know why I'm doing it while it's happening, it just happens. And then with my calculator, I've developed this thing where I am just sitting there tapping on it in sets of exactly nine. Just tap, tap, tap, up to nine, over and over again, and I can't really explain to my supervisor why I'm doing that instead of actually doing the math. 

The weirdest part, though, the thing that really made people start looking at me funny, was the other day. I went over to the copy machine to scan some invoices, and I just... stopped. I stood right there by the copier and held my hands up in the air, just completely held them up by my head, and I stood perfectly still like that for I don't even know how long. I couldn't put them down. I just stayed frozen right there until one of the managers finally walked by and asked if I was okay. Once they asked, I snapped out of it, but it's just really bizarre, and it's starting to really interfere with my days.
=====================================

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It's not... I wouldn't call it an anxiety, exactly. Like, I'm not sitting there terrified that if I don't tap the calculator nine times my car is going to explode or something bad will happen to my family. And it doesn't feel like a ghost or something is physically moving my hands, either. It's more about... well, it's about what the numbers themselves actually mean. 

Because I stare at these spreadsheets all day, right? You really start to see how everything connects when you're doing bookkeeping for hours on end. And lately—and I know it sounds strange to say this out loud to you—but I get completely convinced that certain invoice totals, specific account numbers, and even the due dates on the billing cycles are actually hidden messages about me. Like, they are being sent directly to my desk for a reason. So when I'm lining up those paper receipts all the way across the office carpet, I'm actually trying to lay out the sequence physically so I can read the whole message at once, to see how the dates and totals line up. 

My supervisor, she actually sat down with me the other day because she was getting frustrated, and she pulled up the original vendor files on her computer to show me everything. She went through them line by line, saying, look, this is just a normal entry for printer toner, this is just a standard utility bill. But even when she shows me that, and I'm looking right at the screen with my own two eyes, I still feel this intense... this absolute certainty, I guess, that they are tracking whether I understand the pattern. Like whoever is putting these numbers into the system is watching me to see if I can decode what they're saying. So the tapping on the calculator and the receipts, it's just me trying to figure out the pattern they're testing me on, but it's just so hard to explain all of that to my boss when she just wants the monthly audit finished by three o'clock, you know?

No, honestly, I haven't noticed anything like that. No headaches at all. I mean, sometimes my eyes get a little dry or tired by like, 4:00 PM, just from staring at the dual monitors we have at work—they gave us these new Dell monitors last month and the default brightness is just incredibly intense—but that's just regular eye strain, you know? My mom always used to say that hydration prevents headaches, and I guess she's right, because physically, my head feels completely fine. 

And as for my memory... my memory is actually really, really sharp right now. Like, maybe even sharper than usual? I can remember exactly which row and column every single vendor code is in, and I can recall the exact timestamp on the invoices from three weeks ago, which is exactly why I'm able to notice how all these dates and totals are connecting in the first place. If my memory was bad, or if I was getting forgetful, I'd probably just miss the messages completely. But I remember everything, down to the penny. 

I haven't felt sick, no fevers, no weird pains in my body or anything. I stood right there by the copier and held my hands up in the air, just completely held them up by my head, and I stood perfectly still like that for I don't even know how long. I couldn't put them down. I just stayed frozen right there until one of the managers finally walked by and asked if I was okay. Once they asked, I snapped out of it, but it's just really bizarre, and it's starting to really interfere with my days.\n\nDoctor: When you are doing these repetitive things like lining up the receipts or tapping the calculator, do you feel an internal sense of pressure or anxiety that forces you to do them to prevent something bad from happening, or does it feel like something else is controlling your actions?\n\nPatient: It's not... I wouldn't call it an anxiety, exactly. Like, I'm not sitting there terrified that if I don't tap the calculator nine times my car is going to explode or something bad will happen to my family. And it doesn't feel like a ghost or something is physically moving my hands, either. It's more about... well, it's about what the numbers themselves actually mean. \n\nBecause I stare at these spreadsheets all day, right? You really start to see how everything connects when you're doing bookkeeping for hours on end. And lately—and I know it sounds strange to say this out loud to you—but I get completely convinced that certain invoice totals, specific account numbers, and even the due dates on the billing cycles are actually hidden messages about me. Like, they are being sent directly to my desk for a reason. So when I'm lining up those paper receipts all the way across the office carpet, I'm actually trying to lay out the sequence physically so I can read the whole message at once, to see how the dates and totals line up. \n\nMy supervisor, she actually sat down with me the other day because she was getting frustrated, and she pulled up the original vendor files on her computer to show me everything. She went through them line by line, saying, look, this is just a normal entry for printer toner, this is just a standard utility bill. But even when she shows me that, and I'm looking right at the screen with my own two eyes, I still feel this intense... this absolute certainty, I guess, that they are tracking whether I understand the pattern. Like whoever is putting these numbers into the system is watching me to see if I can decode what they're saying. So the tapping on the calculator and the receipts, it's just me trying to figure out the pattern they're testing me on, but it's just so hard to explain all of that to my boss when she just wants the monthly audit finished by three o'clock, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in your health recently, such as persistent headaches, memory problems, or any new physical symptoms that occurred around the same time these behaviors started?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, I haven't noticed anything like that. No headaches at all. I mean, sometimes my eyes get a little dry or tired by like, 4:00 PM, just from staring at the dual monitors we have at work—they gave us these new Dell monitors last month and the default brightness is just incredibly intense—but that's just regular eye strain, you know? Not a real headache. I don't get migraines or anything like that. I always make sure to keep a big water bottle right by my keyboard, one of those insulated metal ones, so I'm drinking enough water all day. My mom always used to say that hydration prevents headaches, and I guess she's right, because physically, my head feels completely fine. \n\nAnd as for my memory... my memory is actually really, really sharp right now. Like, maybe even sharper than usual? I can remember exactly which row and column every single vendor code is in, and I can recall the exact timestamp on the invoices from three weeks ago, which is exactly why I'm able to notice how all these dates and totals are connecting in the first place. If my memory was bad, or if I was getting forgetful, I'd probably just miss the messages completely. But I remember everything, down to the penny. \n\nI haven't felt sick, no fevers, no weird pains in my body or anything. I still walk around the business park on my thirty-minute lunch break, usually just doing the loop around the employee parking lot and the little retention pond they have out back, and my energy for that is totally normal. My legs feel fine, my breathing is fine. I feel physically healthy, exactly like I always do. It's really just... it's just the things I'm doing with the receipts and the copier, and the numbers. But physically? No, I haven't had any new physical symptoms at all. Everything with my body is totally normal.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you are having these experiences with the numbers and the patterns, do you find that your thoughts or your speech become jumbled or difficult for others to follow, or do you feel like you are speaking and thinking quite clearly?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, see, that's the thing. In my own head? I feel like I am thinking more clearly than I ever have in my entire life. It’s like everything is suddenly illuminated, like a giant web where I can finally see all the strings connecting. But... I guess, when I actually try to say it out loud to other people, they've been getting really frustrated with me. 

Like, my supervisor, for instance. Just the other day—I think it was Tuesday, because it was right after I got back from campus and I always have my accounting ethics class on Tuesday mornings—I was trying to explain to her why one of the quarterly ledgers was off. It was just a tiny discrepancy, really, just a few dollars, but in bookkeeping, every penny matters, right? You have to balance it. So I started explaining the discrepancy to her, but to explain it properly, you really have to understand the whole context of what the numbers are doing. 

So I was talking about the ledger, and then that naturally led me to mention the tuition deadlines for the classes I'm taking, because the dates on the university portal were mirroring the exact billing cycle dates on the ledger. And then, obviously, if you're talking about tuition dates, you have to factor in the new tax rules for educational deductions, which I was just reading about, and those tax percentages perfectly matched the wattage output of the studio lighting we were using in my elective photography class... and those studio lighting numbers were the exact same number patterns I was trying to decode on my calculator! 

So I'm explaining all of this to her, just laying out exactly how the tuition deadlines, the tax rules, the studio lighting, and the number patterns are all completely tied together to explain why the ledger is off. It was a perfectly straight line to me. But she just stared at me. She literally held up her hand, right in the middle of the office, and told me she could not follow a single word I was saying. She said my thoughts were just jumping all over the place and she had absolutely no idea how I got from a basic ledger discrepancy to talking about studio lighting and tax rules. She said it was completely impossible to follow my point. But to me, I didn't jump at all, you know? I didn't skip anything. I just walked straight down the path. It's just that no one else seems to be able to keep up with the steps I'm taking when I try to explain it to them.

So the tapping on the calculator and the receipts, it's just me trying to figure out the pattern they're testing me on, but it's just so hard to explain all of that to my boss when she just wants the monthly audit finished by three o'clock, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in your health recently, such as persistent headaches, memory problems, or any new physical symptoms that occurred around the same time these behaviors started?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, I haven't noticed anything like that. No headaches at all. I mean, sometimes my eyes get a little dry or tired by like, 4:00 PM, just from staring at the dual monitors we have at work—they gave us these new Dell monitors last month and the default brightness is just incredibly intense—but that's just regular eye strain, you know? Not a real headache. I don't get migraines or anything like that. I always make sure to keep a big water bottle right by my keyboard, one of those insulated metal ones, so I'm drinking enough water all day. My mom always used to say that hydration prevents headaches, and I guess she's right, because physically, my head feels completely fine. \n\nAnd as for my memory... my memory is actually really, really sharp right now. Like, maybe even sharper than usual? If my memory was bad, or if I was getting forgetful, I'd probably just miss the messages completely. But I remember everything, down to the penny. \n\nI haven't felt sick, no fevers, no weird pains in my body or anything. In my own head? I feel like I am thinking more clearly than I ever have in my entire life. It’s like everything is suddenly illuminated, like a giant web where I can finally see all the strings connecting. But... I guess, when I actually try to say it out loud to other people, they've been getting really frustrated with me. \n\nLike, my supervisor, for instance. It was just a tiny discrepancy, really, just a few dollars, but in bookkeeping, every penny matters, right? You have to balance it. So I started explaining the discrepancy to her, but to explain it properly, you really have to understand the whole context of what the numbers are doing. \n\nSo I was talking about the ledger, and then that naturally led me to mention the tuition deadlines for the classes I'm taking, because the dates on the university portal were mirroring the exact billing cycle dates on the ledger. It was a perfectly straight line to me. But she just stared at me. She literally held up her hand, right in the middle of the office, and told me she could not follow a single word I was saying. She said my thoughts were just jumping all over the place and she had absolutely no idea how I got from a basic ledger discrepancy to talking about studio lighting and tax rules. She said it was completely impossible to follow my point. But to me, I didn't jump at all, you know? I didn't skip anything. I just walked straight down the path. Well, see, that's the part that really confuses me, because it wasn't like my muscles were locked up or paralyzed or anything like that. Physically, my joints weren't stiff. I could have moved my arms down whenever I wanted to. But I wasn't really caught up in a specific number pattern in my head right then, either. 

Honestly, it’s the main reason I even came in to see you in the first place, because I’ve just started doing all these things at work and at school that completely do not make any sense at all. Like I was telling you before about lining up all the paper receipts all the way across the office floor, or how I sit there tapping the calculator in those specific sets of nine. The copier thing was just... another one of those bizarre things that makes zero sense to me. 

We have this massive Canon imageRUNNER in the breakroom—it’s one of those huge industrial ones that collates and staples and takes up half the wall—and I had gone in there to run the weekly expense reports. And I just ended up standing there by the machine with my hands held straight up in the air. I wasn't physically stuck, and I wasn't decoding a message right that second, I was just... holding them up. I just stood there like that, completely frozen with my hands in the air, for I don't even know how long. It felt like forever, but maybe it was just a few minutes? I didn't move at all until Brenda—she’s our front desk receptionist, she’s been with the company for like fifteen years and always comes in for her mid-morning decaf—she walked into the breakroom and saw me and was just like, "Are you okay?" 

And the second she asked if I was okay, I just put my hands down and grabbed my expense reports like nothing happened. But it really freaked me out afterward, because why would I do that? I have absolutely no idea why I was standing like that. It just doesn't make any logical sense why I've started doing these things.

I always make sure to keep a big water bottle right by my keyboard, one of those insulated metal ones, so I'm drinking enough water all day. My mom always used to say that hydration prevents headaches, and I guess she's right, because physically, my head feels completely fine. \n\nAnd as for my memory... my memory is actually really, really sharp right now. Like, maybe even sharper than usual? I can remember exactly which row and column every single vendor code is in, and I can recall the exact timestamp on the invoices from three weeks ago, which is exactly why I'm able to notice how all these dates and totals are connecting in the first place. If my memory was bad, or if I was getting forgetful, I'd probably just miss the messages completely. But I remember everything, down to the penny. \n\nI haven't felt sick, no fevers, no weird pains in my body or anything. I still walk around the business park on my thirty-minute lunch break, usually just doing the loop around the employee parking lot and the little retention pond they have out back, and my energy for that is totally normal. My legs feel fine, my breathing is fine. I feel physically healthy, exactly like I always do. It's really just... it's just the things I'm doing with the receipts and the copier, and the numbers. But physically? No, I haven't had any new physical symptoms at all. Everything with my body is totally normal.\n\nDoctor: When you are having these experiences with the numbers and the patterns, do you find that your thoughts or your speech become jumbled or difficult for others to follow, or do you feel like you are speaking and thinking quite clearly?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the thing. In my own head? I feel like I am thinking more clearly than I ever have in my entire life. It’s like everything is suddenly illuminated, like a giant web where I can finally see all the strings connecting. But... I guess, when I actually try to say it out loud to other people, they've been getting really frustrated with me. \n\nLike, my supervisor, for instance. Just the other day—I think it was Tuesday, because it was right after I got back from campus and I always have my accounting ethics class on Tuesday mornings—I was trying to explain to her why one of the quarterly ledgers was off. It was just a tiny discrepancy, really, just a few dollars, but in bookkeeping, every penny matters, right? You have to balance it. So I started explaining the discrepancy to her, but to explain it properly, you really have to understand the whole context of what the numbers are doing. \n\nSo I was talking about the ledger, and then that naturally led me to mention the tuition deadlines for the classes I'm taking, because the dates on the university portal were mirroring the exact billing cycle dates on the ledger. And then, obviously, if you're talking about tuition dates, you have to factor in the new tax rules for educational deductions, which I was just reading about, and those tax percentages perfectly matched the wattage output of the studio lighting we were using in my elective photography class... and those studio lighting numbers were the exact same number patterns I was trying to decode on my calculator! \n\nSo I'm explaining all of this to her, just laying out exactly how the tuition deadlines, the tax rules, the studio lighting, and the number patterns are all completely tied together to explain why the ledger is off. It was a perfectly straight line to me. But she just stared at me. She literally held up her hand, right in the middle of the office, and told me she could not follow a single word I was saying. She said my thoughts were just jumping all over the place and she had absolutely no idea how I got from a basic ledger discrepancy to talking about studio lighting and tax rules. She said it was completely impossible to follow my point. But to me, I didn't jump at all, you know? I didn't skip anything. I just walked straight down the path. It's just that no one else seems to be able to keep up with the steps I'm taking when I try to explain it to them.\n\nDoctor: When you stood frozen at the copy machine with your hands in the air, were you holding that position because you were caught up in one of your patterns, or did your body feel like it was stuck in that position regardless of what you were thinking about?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the part that really confuses me, because it wasn't like my muscles were locked up or paralyzed or anything like that. Physically, my joints weren't stiff. I could have moved my arms down whenever I wanted to. But I wasn't really caught up in a specific number pattern in my head right then, either. \n\nHonestly, it’s the main reason I even came in to see you in the first place, because I’ve just started doing all these things at work and at school that completely do not make any sense at all. Like I was telling you before about lining up all the paper receipts all the way across the office floor, or how I sit there tapping the calculator in those specific sets of nine. The copier thing was just... another one of those bizarre things that makes zero sense to me. \n\nWe have this massive Canon imageRUNNER in the breakroom—it’s one of those huge industrial ones that collates and staples and takes up half the wall—and I had gone in there to run the weekly expense reports. And I just ended up standing there by the machine with my hands held straight up in the air. I wasn't physically stuck, and I wasn't decoding a message right that second, I was just... holding them up. I just stood there like that, completely frozen with my hands in the air, for I don't even know how long. It felt like forever, but maybe it was just a few minutes? I didn't move at all until Brenda—she’s our front desk receptionist, she’s been with the company for like fifteen years and always comes in for her mid-morning decaf—she walked into the breakroom and saw me and was just like, \"Are you okay?\" \n\nAnd the second she asked if I was okay, I just put my hands down and grabbed my expense reports like nothing happened. But it really freaked me out afterward, because why would I do that? I have absolutely no idea why I was standing like that. It just doesn't make any logical sense why I've started doing these things.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow long have you been experiencing these patterns with the numbers and these episodes of freezing?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

Well, if I really sit down and try to trace it all back to the very beginning, I'd say this has all been building up for about ten months now. Yeah, ten months. 

It started right around the time I decided to enroll in these evening accounting courses. I've been working as a bookkeeping clerk since I finished high school, and I really like the company, but I figured if I ever want to move up to a senior accountant role or maybe even an auditor position someday, I really need to get the actual degree on paper to back up all the practical experience I already have. So, about ten months ago, I enrolled at the satellite campus—it's super convenient, actually, just about a fifteen-minute drive from my office if the traffic on I-95 isn't too backed up. 

But taking on that course load while keeping my full-time bookkeeping job meant my schedule just became incredibly packed. I'd be at the office working with spreadsheets and receipts from 8:00 AM to 5:00 PM, and then I'd barely have time to grab a quick sandwich—usually just a turkey club from the deli downstairs—before driving straight to campus for classes that ran from 6:00 to 9:00 PM. And it was right when that whole grueling schedule started, ten months ago, that I first started noticing the numbers kind of... jumping out at me. 

At first, it was just little things, you know? Like I'd notice the page numbers in my intermediate accounting textbook were perfectly mirroring the vendor invoice numbers I had just been filing at work that afternoon. But then it just kept building and building over these past ten months, getting more intense, until I'm noticing the tuition dates and the tax percentages and laying the receipts all over the floor. And then the freezing thing at the copier... that just feels like the culmination of it all. It all just traces back to that exact point ten months ago when I started trying to balance the job and the evening classes. I really thought I could handle the workload because I've always been so good with numbers, but yeah... ten months is exactly when this whole thing really started taking root.

But recently, I've started doing these things at work and at school that just... they completely do not make sense. Even to me. \n\nLike, I'll be sitting at my desk with a stack of expense reports, and instead of just entering the data into the spreadsheet like a normal person, I'll take all the little paper receipts and start lining them up, end to end, all the way across the office floor. I don't even really know why I'm doing it while it's happening, it just happens. And then with my calculator, I've developed this thing where I am just sitting there tapping on it in sets of exactly nine. I went over to the copy machine to scan some invoices, and I just... stopped. Once they asked, I snapped out of it, but it's just really bizarre, and it's starting to really interfere with my days.\n\nDoctor: When you are doing these repetitive things like lining up the receipts or tapping the calculator, do you feel an internal sense of pressure or anxiety that forces you to do them to prevent something bad from happening, or does it feel like something else is controlling your actions?\n\nPatient: It's not... I wouldn't call it an anxiety, exactly. Like, I'm not sitting there terrified that if I don't tap the calculator nine times my car is going to explode or something bad will happen to my family. And then, obviously, if you're talking about tuition dates, you have to factor in the new tax rules for educational deductions, which I was just reading about, and those tax percentages perfectly matched the wattage output of the studio lighting we were using in my elective photography class... and those studio lighting numbers were the exact same number patterns I was trying to decode on my calculator! \n\nSo I'm explaining all of this to her, just laying out exactly how the tuition deadlines, the tax rules, the studio lighting, and the number patterns are all completely tied together to explain why the ledger is off. It was a perfectly straight line to me. But she just stared at me. She literally held up her hand, right in the middle of the office, and told me she could not follow a single word I was saying. She said my thoughts were just jumping all over the place and she had absolutely no idea how I got from a basic ledger discrepancy to talking about studio lighting and tax rules. She said it was completely impossible to follow my point. But to me, I didn't jump at all, you know? I didn't skip anything. I just walked straight down the path. It's just that no one else seems to be able to keep up with the steps I'm taking when I try to explain it to them.\n\nDoctor: When you stood frozen at the copy machine with your hands in the air, were you holding that position because you were caught up in one of your patterns, or did your body feel like it was stuck in that position regardless of what you were thinking about?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the part that really confuses me, because it wasn't like my muscles were locked up or paralyzed or anything like that. Physically, my joints weren't stiff. I could have moved my arms down whenever I wanted to. But I wasn't really caught up in a specific number pattern in my head right then, either. \n\nHonestly, it’s the main reason I even came in to see you in the first place, because I’ve just started doing all these things at work and at school that completely do not make any sense at all. Like I was telling you before about lining up all the paper receipts all the way across the office floor, or how I sit there tapping the calculator in those specific sets of nine. The copier thing was just... another one of those bizarre things that makes zero sense to me. \n\nWe have this massive Canon imageRUNNER in the breakroom—it’s one of those huge industrial ones that collates and staples and takes up half the wall—and I had gone in there to run the weekly expense reports. And I just ended up standing there by the machine with my hands held straight up in the air. I wasn't physically stuck, and I wasn't decoding a message right that second, I was just... holding them up. I just stood there like that, completely frozen with my hands in the air, for I don't even know how long. It felt like forever, but maybe it was just a few minutes? I didn't move at all until Brenda—she’s our front desk receptionist, she’s been with the company for like fifteen years and always comes in for her mid-morning decaf—she walked into the breakroom and saw me and was just like, \"Are you okay?\" \n\nAnd the second she asked if I was okay, I just put my hands down and grabbed my expense reports like nothing happened. But it really freaked me out afterward, because why would I do that? I have absolutely no idea why I was standing like that. It just doesn't make any logical sense why I've started doing these things.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these patterns with the numbers and these episodes of freezing?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really sit down and try to trace it all back to the very beginning, I'd say this has all been building up for about ten months now. Yeah, ten months. \n\nIt started right around the time I decided to enroll in these evening accounting courses. I've been working as a bookkeeping clerk since I finished high school, and I really like the company, but I figured if I ever want to move up to a senior accountant role or maybe even an auditor position someday, I really need to get the actual degree on paper to back up all the practical experience I already have. So, about ten months ago, I enrolled at the satellite campus—it's super convenient, actually, just about a fifteen-minute drive from my office if the traffic on I-95 isn't too backed up. \n\nBut taking on that course load while keeping my full-time bookkeeping job meant my schedule just became incredibly packed. I'd be at the office working with spreadsheets and receipts from 8:00 AM to 5:00 PM, and then I'd barely have time to grab a quick sandwich—usually just a turkey club from the deli downstairs—before driving straight to campus for classes that ran from 6:00 to 9:00 PM. And it was right when that whole grueling schedule started, ten months ago, that I first started noticing the numbers kind of... jumping out at me. \n\nAt first, it was just little things, you know? Like I'd notice the page numbers in my intermediate accounting textbook were perfectly mirroring the vendor invoice numbers I had just been filing at work that afternoon. But then it just kept building and building over these past ten months, getting more intense, until I'm noticing the tuition dates and the tax percentages and laying the receipts all over the floor. And then the freezing thing at the copier... that just feels like the culmination of it all. It all just traces back to that exact point ten months ago when I started trying to balance the job and the evening classes. I really thought I could handle the workload because I've always been so good with numbers, but yeah... ten months is exactly when this whole thing really started taking root.\n\n[Last Question]\nYou mentioned these experiences have been occurring for ten months; have you had any periods during these ten months where you felt completely back to your normal self, or have these symptoms been present consistently throughout that entire time?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 22 / bookkeeping_accounting_or_auditing_clerk / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, see, that's a really interesting question, and it's actually something I've tried to figure out myself. Because, you know, being a bookkeeper, my whole life is about tracking things, right? Everything has to have a date, a timestamp, a paper trail. I even have this really detailed daily planner—it’s a navy blue leather one I got at OfficeMax right before the fall semester started—and I’ve actually sat down at my kitchen table and tried to flip back through the pages to see if I could find a gap. Like, I thought maybe during the week of Thanksgiving, or maybe during the winter break between semesters? Because during winter break I wasn't driving to campus every night, though I was doing end-of-year tax prep at the office, which is its own kind of chaos, honestly, with everyone panicking about W-2s and 1099s... 

But... to actually answer your question about whether I felt completely back to my normal self at any point? I honestly just don't know. I really couldn't tell you for sure. It’s like, when you’re working a full-time job and taking evening classes, your whole life just turns into this massive, exhausting blur. You wake up at 6:00 AM, you make the coffee, you fight the traffic on I-95, you stare at spreadsheets for eight hours, you rush to class, you come home, and you do it all over again. It’s just this endless loop. 

So trying to look back and pinpoint if there was a specific week, or even just a few days, where the numbers weren't jumping out at me, or where I wasn't getting distracted by the patterns... I just haven't been tracking my own thoughts the way I track the accounts at work. I wish I could give you a definitive yes or no, but I really just haven't noticed if there was a clean break in it all. I'm just not sure.

But physically? No, I haven't had any new physical symptoms at all. Everything with my body is totally normal.\n\nDoctor: When you are having these experiences with the numbers and the patterns, do you find that your thoughts or your speech become jumbled or difficult for others to follow, or do you feel like you are speaking and thinking quite clearly?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the thing. In my own head? I feel like I am thinking more clearly than I ever have in my entire life. It’s like everything is suddenly illuminated, like a giant web where I can finally see all the strings connecting. But... I guess, when I actually try to say it out loud to other people, they've been getting really frustrated with me. \n\nLike, my supervisor, for instance. Just the other day—I think it was Tuesday, because it was right after I got back from campus and I always have my accounting ethics class on Tuesday mornings—I was trying to explain to her why one of the quarterly ledgers was off. It was just a tiny discrepancy, really, just a few dollars, but in bookkeeping, every penny matters, right? You have to balance it. So I started explaining the discrepancy to her, but to explain it properly, you really have to understand the whole context of what the numbers are doing. \n\nSo I was talking about the ledger, and then that naturally led me to mention the tuition deadlines for the classes I'm taking, because the dates on the university portal were mirroring the exact billing cycle dates on the ledger. And then, obviously, if you're talking about tuition dates, you have to factor in the new tax rules for educational deductions, which I was just reading about, and those tax percentages perfectly matched the wattage output of the studio lighting we were using in my elective photography class... and those studio lighting numbers were the exact same number patterns I was trying to decode on my calculator! \n\nSo I'm explaining all of this to her, just laying out exactly how the tuition deadlines, the tax rules, the studio lighting, and the number patterns are all completely tied together to explain why the ledger is off. It was a perfectly straight line to me. But she just stared at me. She literally held up her hand, right in the middle of the office, and told me she could not follow a single word I was saying. She said my thoughts were just jumping all over the place and she had absolutely no idea how I got from a basic ledger discrepancy to talking about studio lighting and tax rules. She said it was completely impossible to follow my point. But to me, I didn't jump at all, you know? I didn't skip anything. I just walked straight down the path. It's just that no one else seems to be able to keep up with the steps I'm taking when I try to explain it to them.\n\nDoctor: When you stood frozen at the copy machine with your hands in the air, were you holding that position because you were caught up in one of your patterns, or did your body feel like it was stuck in that position regardless of what you were thinking about?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the part that really confuses me, because it wasn't like my muscles were locked up or paralyzed or anything like that. Physically, my joints weren't stiff. I could have moved my arms down whenever I wanted to. But I wasn't really caught up in a specific number pattern in my head right then, either. \n\nHonestly, it’s the main reason I even came in to see you in the first place, because I’ve just started doing all these things at work and at school that completely do not make any sense at all. Like I was telling you before about lining up all the paper receipts all the way across the office floor, or how I sit there tapping the calculator in those specific sets of nine. The copier thing was just... another one of those bizarre things that makes zero sense to me. \n\nWe have this massive Canon imageRUNNER in the breakroom—it’s one of those huge industrial ones that collates and staples and takes up half the wall—and I had gone in there to run the weekly expense reports. And I just ended up standing there by the machine with my hands held straight up in the air. I wasn't physically stuck, and I wasn't decoding a message right that second, I was just... holding them up. I just stood there like that, completely frozen with my hands in the air, for I don't even know how long. It felt like forever, but maybe it was just a few minutes? I didn't move at all until Brenda—she’s our front desk receptionist, she’s been with the company for like fifteen years and always comes in for her mid-morning decaf—she walked into the breakroom and saw me and was just like, \"Are you okay?\" \n\nAnd the second she asked if I was okay, I just put my hands down and grabbed my expense reports like nothing happened. But it really freaked me out afterward, because why would I do that? I have absolutely no idea why I was standing like that. It just doesn't make any logical sense why I've started doing these things.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these patterns with the numbers and these episodes of freezing?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really sit down and try to trace it all back to the very beginning, I'd say this has all been building up for about ten months now. Yeah, ten months. \n\nIt started right around the time I decided to enroll in these evening accounting courses. I've been working as a bookkeeping clerk since I finished high school, and I really like the company, but I figured if I ever want to move up to a senior accountant role or maybe even an auditor position someday, I really need to get the actual degree on paper to back up all the practical experience I already have. So, about ten months ago, I enrolled at the satellite campus—it's super convenient, actually, just about a fifteen-minute drive from my office if the traffic on I-95 isn't too backed up. \n\nBut taking on that course load while keeping my full-time bookkeeping job meant my schedule just became incredibly packed. I'd be at the office working with spreadsheets and receipts from 8:00 AM to 5:00 PM, and then I'd barely have time to grab a quick sandwich—usually just a turkey club from the deli downstairs—before driving straight to campus for classes that ran from 6:00 to 9:00 PM. And it was right when that whole grueling schedule started, ten months ago, that I first started noticing the numbers kind of... jumping out at me. \n\nAt first, it was just little things, you know? Like I'd notice the page numbers in my intermediate accounting textbook were perfectly mirroring the vendor invoice numbers I had just been filing at work that afternoon. But then it just kept building and building over these past ten months, getting more intense, until I'm noticing the tuition dates and the tax percentages and laying the receipts all over the floor. And then the freezing thing at the copier... that just feels like the culmination of it all. It all just traces back to that exact point ten months ago when I started trying to balance the job and the evening classes. I really thought I could handle the workload because I've always been so good with numbers, but yeah... ten months is exactly when this whole thing really started taking root.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned these experiences have been occurring for ten months; have you had any periods during these ten months where you felt completely back to your normal self, or have these symptoms been present consistently throughout that entire time?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's a really interesting question, and it's actually something I've tried to figure out myself. Because, you know, being a bookkeeper, my whole life is about tracking things, right? Everything has to have a date, a timestamp, a paper trail. I even have this really detailed daily planner—it’s a navy blue leather one I got at OfficeMax right before the fall semester started—and I’ve actually sat down at my kitchen table and tried to flip back through the pages to see if I could find a gap. Like, I thought maybe during the week of Thanksgiving, or maybe during the winter break between semesters? Because during winter break I wasn't driving to campus every night, though I was doing end-of-year tax prep at the office, which is its own kind of chaos, honestly, with everyone panicking about W-2s and 1099s... \n\nBut... to actually answer your question about whether I felt completely back to my normal self at any point? I honestly just don't know. I really couldn't tell you for sure. It’s like, when you’re working a full-time job and taking evening classes, your whole life just turns into this massive, exhausting blur. You wake up at 6:00 AM, you make the coffee, you fight the traffic on I-95, you stare at spreadsheets for eight hours, you rush to class, you come home, and you do it all over again. It’s just this endless loop. \n\nSo trying to look back and pinpoint if there was a specific week, or even just a few days, where the numbers weren't jumping out at me, or where I wasn't getting distracted by the patterns... I just haven't been tracking my own thoughts the way I track the accounts at work. I wish I could give you a definitive yes or no, but I really just haven't noticed if there was a clean break in it all. I'm just not sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I'm just not sure.\n\n[Last Question]\nBeyond the patterns you see in the numbers, have you ever heard any voices or sounds that other people around you couldn't hear?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 22 / bookkeeping_accounting_or_auditing_clerk / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Hallucinations\nmanifestation: \"Sometimes when I am alone at my desk or walking home from class, I hear a man's voice say things like 'check line seven' or 'you missed the sign,' clear enough that I look around even though nobody is there.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, um... it's funny you ask that, because, yeah. Actually, yes. And it's honestly been really, really unsettling for me, to be completely honest with you. 

Like, I'll be sitting at my desk at work—my cubicle is all the way in the back corner of the accounting department, right next to these massive metal filing cabinets, so usually it's pretty quiet back there. Especially if I stay a little late to finish up the daily reconciliations and most of the office has already gone home for the day. It'll just be me, completely alone, and the hum of the HVAC system overhead. And then, out of absolutely nowhere, I'll hear this voice. It's a man's voice. Very distinct, kind of deep and authoritative, almost like a supervisor or maybe one of my accounting professors? And he'll say something really specific, like, "check line seven." 

And I mean, it is so incredibly clear. It sounds exactly like someone is standing right over my left shoulder, just leaning in and looking at my dual monitors. I literally spin my chair around—it's one of those mesh ergonomic chairs so it spins really fast—and I'll be fully expecting to see Mr. Henderson, our department manager, or maybe one of the night janitors standing right there waiting for me to answer. But... there's absolutely nobody there. Just the empty cubicles and the fluorescent lights. 

And it's not just at the office, either, which is the part that really gets me. It happens when I'm walking home, or well, walking to my car after my evening classes. The parking lot at the satellite campus is kind of poorly lit on the south side where the commuter students have to park, so it's already a little desolate when I'm walking out at 9:15 PM after a three-hour lecture. I'll just be walking, carrying my heavy backpack, thinking about the drive home on I-95, and I'll hear that exact same man's voice say, "you missed the sign." Just like that. "You missed the sign." 

And I'll stop dead in my tracks on the pavement and look all around the parking lot, peering between the parked cars, thinking maybe some other student is shouting at me or trying to warn me about something I stepped on or walked past. But the lot is completely deserted. Just me and the flickering streetlights. It really messes with my head because it sounds so physically real, you know? Like, the actual acoustics of it sound like a real, physical person speaking into the open air right next to me, not just a random thought inside my own head. So I'm looking around like an idiot for someone who just isn't there.

My legs feel fine, my breathing is fine. I feel physically healthy, exactly like I always do. It's really just... it's just the things I'm doing with the receipts and the copier, and the numbers. But physically? No, I haven't had any new physical symptoms at all. Everything with my body is totally normal.\n\nDoctor: When you are having these experiences with the numbers and the patterns, do you find that your thoughts or your speech become jumbled or difficult for others to follow, or do you feel like you are speaking and thinking quite clearly?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the thing. In my own head? I feel like I am thinking more clearly than I ever have in my entire life. It’s like everything is suddenly illuminated, like a giant web where I can finally see all the strings connecting. But... So I started explaining the discrepancy to her, but to explain it properly, you really have to understand the whole context of what the numbers are doing. \n\nSo I was talking about the ledger, and then that naturally led me to mention the tuition deadlines for the classes I'm taking, because the dates on the university portal were mirroring the exact billing cycle dates on the ledger. And then, obviously, if you're talking about tuition dates, you have to factor in the new tax rules for educational deductions, which I was just reading about, and those tax percentages perfectly matched the wattage output of the studio lighting we were using in my elective photography class... and those studio lighting numbers were the exact same number patterns I was trying to decode on my calculator! \n\nSo I'm explaining all of this to her, just laying out exactly how the tuition deadlines, the tax rules, the studio lighting, and the number patterns are all completely tied together to explain why the ledger is off. She said my thoughts were just jumping all over the place and she had absolutely no idea how I got from a basic ledger discrepancy to talking about studio lighting and tax rules. She said it was completely impossible to follow my point. But to me, I didn't jump at all, you know? I didn't skip anything. I just walked straight down the path. It's just that no one else seems to be able to keep up with the steps I'm taking when I try to explain it to them.\n\nDoctor: When you stood frozen at the copy machine with your hands in the air, were you holding that position because you were caught up in one of your patterns, or did your body feel like it was stuck in that position regardless of what you were thinking about?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the part that really confuses me, because it wasn't like my muscles were locked up or paralyzed or anything like that. I didn't move at all until Brenda—she’s our front desk receptionist, she’s been with the company for like fifteen years and always comes in for her mid-morning decaf—she walked into the breakroom and saw me and was just like, \"Are you okay?\" \n\nAnd the second she asked if I was okay, I just put my hands down and grabbed my expense reports like nothing happened. It just doesn't make any logical sense why I've started doing these things.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these patterns with the numbers and these episodes of freezing?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really sit down and try to trace it all back to the very beginning, I'd say this has all been building up for about ten months now. So, about ten months ago, I enrolled at the satellite campus—it's super convenient, actually, just about a fifteen-minute drive from my office if the traffic on I-95 isn't too backed up. \n\nBut taking on that course load while keeping my full-time bookkeeping job meant my schedule just became incredibly packed. And then the freezing thing at the copier... that just feels like the culmination of it all. I really thought I could handle the workload because I've always been so good with numbers, but yeah... ten months is exactly when this whole thing really started taking root.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned these experiences have been occurring for ten months; have you had any periods during these ten months where you felt completely back to your normal self, or have these symptoms been present consistently throughout that entire time?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's a really interesting question, and it's actually something I've tried to figure out myself. I even have this really detailed daily planner—it’s a navy blue leather one I got at OfficeMax right before the fall semester started—and I’ve actually sat down at my kitchen table and tried to flip back through the pages to see if I could find a gap. Because during winter break I wasn't driving to campus every night, though I was doing end-of-year tax prep at the office, which is its own kind of chaos, honestly, with everyone panicking about W-2s and 1099s... \n\nBut... to actually answer your question about whether I felt completely back to my normal self at any point? I honestly just don't know. I really couldn't tell you for sure. She said my thoughts were just jumping all over the place and she had absolutely no idea how I got from a basic ledger discrepancy to talking about studio lighting and tax rules. She said it was completely impossible to follow my point. But to me, I didn't jump at all, you know? I didn't skip anything. I just walked straight down the path. It's just that no one else seems to be able to keep up with the steps I'm taking when I try to explain it to them.\n\nDoctor: When you stood frozen at the copy machine with your hands in the air, were you holding that position because you were caught up in one of your patterns, or did your body feel like it was stuck in that position regardless of what you were thinking about?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the part that really confuses me, because it wasn't like my muscles were locked up or paralyzed or anything like that. Physically, my joints weren't stiff. I could have moved my arms down whenever I wanted to. But I wasn't really caught up in a specific number pattern in my head right then, either. \n\nHonestly, it’s the main reason I even came in to see you in the first place, because I’ve just started doing all these things at work and at school that completely do not make any sense at all. Like I was telling you before about lining up all the paper receipts all the way across the office floor, or how I sit there tapping the calculator in those specific sets of nine. The copier thing was just... another one of those bizarre things that makes zero sense to me. \n\nWe have this massive Canon imageRUNNER in the breakroom—it’s one of those huge industrial ones that collates and staples and takes up half the wall—and I had gone in there to run the weekly expense reports. And I just ended up standing there by the machine with my hands held straight up in the air. I wasn't physically stuck, and I wasn't decoding a message right that second, I was just... holding them up. I just stood there like that, completely frozen with my hands in the air, for I don't even know how long. It felt like forever, but maybe it was just a few minutes? I didn't move at all until Brenda—she’s our front desk receptionist, she’s been with the company for like fifteen years and always comes in for her mid-morning decaf—she walked into the breakroom and saw me and was just like, \"Are you okay?\" \n\nAnd the second she asked if I was okay, I just put my hands down and grabbed my expense reports like nothing happened. But it really freaked me out afterward, because why would I do that? I have absolutely no idea why I was standing like that. It just doesn't make any logical sense why I've started doing these things.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these patterns with the numbers and these episodes of freezing?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really sit down and try to trace it all back to the very beginning, I'd say this has all been building up for about ten months now. Yeah, ten months. \n\nIt started right around the time I decided to enroll in these evening accounting courses. I've been working as a bookkeeping clerk since I finished high school, and I really like the company, but I figured if I ever want to move up to a senior accountant role or maybe even an auditor position someday, I really need to get the actual degree on paper to back up all the practical experience I already have. So, about ten months ago, I enrolled at the satellite campus—it's super convenient, actually, just about a fifteen-minute drive from my office if the traffic on I-95 isn't too backed up. \n\nBut taking on that course load while keeping my full-time bookkeeping job meant my schedule just became incredibly packed. I'd be at the office working with spreadsheets and receipts from 8:00 AM to 5:00 PM, and then I'd barely have time to grab a quick sandwich—usually just a turkey club from the deli downstairs—before driving straight to campus for classes that ran from 6:00 to 9:00 PM. And it was right when that whole grueling schedule started, ten months ago, that I first started noticing the numbers kind of... jumping out at me. \n\nAt first, it was just little things, you know? Like I'd notice the page numbers in my intermediate accounting textbook were perfectly mirroring the vendor invoice numbers I had just been filing at work that afternoon. But then it just kept building and building over these past ten months, getting more intense, until I'm noticing the tuition dates and the tax percentages and laying the receipts all over the floor. And then the freezing thing at the copier... that just feels like the culmination of it all. It all just traces back to that exact point ten months ago when I started trying to balance the job and the evening classes. I really thought I could handle the workload because I've always been so good with numbers, but yeah... ten months is exactly when this whole thing really started taking root.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned these experiences have been occurring for ten months; have you had any periods during these ten months where you felt completely back to your normal self, or have these symptoms been present consistently throughout that entire time?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's a really interesting question, and it's actually something I've tried to figure out myself. Because, you know, being a bookkeeper, my whole life is about tracking things, right? Everything has to have a date, a timestamp, a paper trail. I even have this really detailed daily planner—it’s a navy blue leather one I got at OfficeMax right before the fall semester started—and I’ve actually sat down at my kitchen table and tried to flip back through the pages to see if I could find a gap. Like, I thought maybe during the week of Thanksgiving, or maybe during the winter break between semesters? Because during winter break I wasn't driving to campus every night, though I was doing end-of-year tax prep at the office, which is its own kind of chaos, honestly, with everyone panicking about W-2s and 1099s... \n\nBut... to actually answer your question about whether I felt completely back to my normal self at any point? I honestly just don't know. I really couldn't tell you for sure. It’s like, when you’re working a full-time job and taking evening classes, your whole life just turns into this massive, exhausting blur. You wake up at 6:00 AM, you make the coffee, you fight the traffic on I-95, you stare at spreadsheets for eight hours, you rush to class, you come home, and you do it all over again. It’s just this endless loop. \n\nSo trying to look back and pinpoint if there was a specific week, or even just a few days, where the numbers weren't jumping out at me, or where I wasn't getting distracted by the patterns... I just haven't been tracking my own thoughts the way I track the accounts at work. I wish I could give you a definitive yes or no, but I really just haven't noticed if there was a clean break in it all. I'm just not sure.\n\nDoctor: Beyond the patterns you see in the numbers, have you ever heard any voices or sounds that other people around you couldn't hear?\n\nPatient: Well, um... it's funny you ask that, because, yeah. Actually, yes. And it's honestly been really, really unsettling for me, to be completely honest with you. \n\nLike, I'll be sitting at my desk at work—my cubicle is all the way in the back corner of the accounting department, right next to these massive metal filing cabinets, so usually it's pretty quiet back there. Especially if I stay a little late to finish up the daily reconciliations and most of the office has already gone home for the day. It'll just be me, completely alone, and the hum of the HVAC system overhead. And then, out of absolutely nowhere, I'll hear this voice. It's a man's voice. Very distinct, kind of deep and authoritative, almost like a supervisor or maybe one of my accounting professors? And he'll say something really specific, like, \"check line seven.\" \n\nAnd I mean, it is so incredibly clear. It sounds exactly like someone is standing right over my left shoulder, just leaning in and looking at my dual monitors. I literally spin my chair around—it's one of those mesh ergonomic chairs so it spins really fast—and I'll be fully expecting to see Mr. Henderson, our department manager, or maybe one of the night janitors standing right there waiting for me to answer. But... there's absolutely nobody there. Just the empty cubicles and the fluorescent lights. \n\nAnd it's not just at the office, either, which is the part that really gets me. It happens when I'm walking home, or well, walking to my car after my evening classes. The parking lot at the satellite campus is kind of poorly lit on the south side where the commuter students have to park, so it's already a little desolate when I'm walking out at 9:15 PM after a three-hour lecture. I'll just be walking, carrying my heavy backpack, thinking about the drive home on I-95, and I'll hear that exact same man's voice say, \"you missed the sign.\" Just like that. \"You missed the sign.\" \n\nAnd I'll stop dead in my tracks on the pavement and look all around the parking lot, peering between the parked cars, thinking maybe some other student is shouting at me or trying to warn me about something I stepped on or walked past. But the lot is completely deserted. Just me and the flickering streetlights. It really messes with my head because it sounds so physically real, you know? Like, the actual acoustics of it sound like a real, physical person speaking into the open air right next to me, not just a random thought inside my own head. So I'm looking around like an idiot for someone who just isn't there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

But... there's absolutely nobody there. Just the empty cubicles and the fluorescent lights. \n\nAnd it's not just at the office, either, which is the part that really gets me. It happens when I'm walking home, or well, walking to my car after my evening classes. The parking lot at the satellite campus is kind of poorly lit on the south side where the commuter students have to park, so it's already a little desolate when I'm walking out at 9:15 PM after a three-hour lecture. I'll just be walking, carrying my heavy backpack, thinking about the drive home on I-95, and I'll hear that exact same man's voice say, \"you missed the sign.\" Just like that. \"You missed the sign.\" \n\nAnd I'll stop dead in my tracks on the pavement and look all around the parking lot, peering between the parked cars, thinking maybe some other student is shouting at me or trying to warn me about something I stepped on or walked past. But the lot is completely deserted. Just me and the flickering streetlights. It really messes with my head because it sounds so physically real, you know? Like, the actual acoustics of it sound like a real, physical person speaking into the open air right next to me, not just a random thought inside my own head. So I'm looking around like an idiot for someone who just isn't there.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you have these moments where you freeze, such as at the copy machine, do you find that your body remains in that position even if someone were to try to move your arm, or do you feel a sense of 'waxy flexibility' where your limbs stay exactly where they are placed?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 22 / bookkeeping_accounting_or_auditing_clerk / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I have started doing things at work and school that do not make sense, like lining receipts across the floor, tapping the calculator in sets of nine, or standing by the copier with my hands held up until someone asks if I am okay.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, um... nobody has actually ever tried to physically grab my arm or move my limbs around when it happens, so I couldn't really tell you about that... that waxy thing you mentioned. What actually happens is, I'll just be standing there by the big Xerox machine—it's the heavy-duty one down the hall from my cubicle, right past the employee breakroom—and I'll just have my hands held up in the air. Like they're just suspended right over the control panel. And I'll just stay exactly like that, totally frozen, until someone finally walks into the copy room and asks if I'm okay. Usually it's Brenda from HR, or maybe one of the other clerks asking if the toner is out again, and the second they ask if I'm alright, it's like the spell breaks and I can finally put my arms down. 

But honestly, that's really the whole reason I finally came in to see someone, because I've started doing all these things at work and even at school that just absolutely do not make any sense. It's not just the freezing. Like, I'll take a whole stack of vendor receipts—and these are important source documents, you know, they have to be filed very carefully for audit purposes—but instead of putting them in the filing cabinet, I'll just start lining them all across the floor of my cubicle. Just laying them out in these long rows on the carpet for no logical reason. 

Or I'll be sitting at my desk, trying to run a trial balance, and I'll just start tapping my calculator... specifically in sets of nine. I'll just sit there tapping the keys, one-two-three-four-five-six-seven-eight-nine, over and over again before I can enter the next string of numbers. It's incredibly frustrating because I'm usually so efficient and I have strict deadlines for the end-of-month close, but I just get stuck doing these completely irrational things. So, yeah, no one is really moving my arms for me, I just get stuck doing these bizarre routines or standing with my hands held up at the copier until someone notices and checks on me.

It’s just this endless loop. \n\nSo trying to look back and pinpoint if there was a specific week, or even just a few days, where the numbers weren't jumping out at me, or where I wasn't getting distracted by the patterns...
